height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 3035.73999pt}

As we have seen in the Remark
8.6
, for an admissible
\lambda
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is a simple
Y(\gl_{n})
submodule of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(n,k)
. According to Theorem
8.4
the map
p_{N}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N+n}(\lambda)\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda^{\natural\shortdownarrow})
is a Yangian module isomorphism for a cuttable
\lambda
. Taking inverse limit, we arrive at the following decomposition result.

Theorem 9.3
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{(r)}_{\infty}(n,k)
decomposes into simple
Y(\gl_{n})
-modules:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{(r)}_{\infty}(n,k)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\infty}\\
\lambda_{j}=-k\lfloor\frac{j-1-r}{n}\rfloor\text{ for }j\gg 0.\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda).

(9.21)

The Drinfeld polynomials of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda)
are

\displaystyle P_{m}(u)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(i,j)$ is a top box of}\\
\text{a height $m$ column}\\
\text{in }\mathrm{SYD}(\lambda/\lambda^{\shortdownarrow})\end{subarray}}(u-r+i-j),\quad m=1,\cdots,n-1.

(9.22)

The eigenvector decomposition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda)
with respect to quantum minors
\{\widetilde{A}_{m}(u)\}_{1\leq m\leq n}
is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\in\mathrm{GT}^{(r)}_{\infty}(n)\\
\Lambda_{1}=\lambda\end{subarray}}V_{\Lambda}.

(9.23)

Proof.

The decomposition (
9.21
) follows from taking inverse limit, namely
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda)=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\leftarrow\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\\
L\end{subarray}}{\lim}\>\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{nL+r}(\lambda)
. The eigenvector decomposition (
9.23
) follows from Theorem
9.2
. The Drinfeld polynomials (
9.22
) essentially follow from
[
63
, Corollary 2.13]
. One should be careful that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is obtained from
V_{\lambda/\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}
by first taking the dual, then shifting spectral parameter, finally multiplying a function
f(u)
. In principal one can chase along the Yangian automorphisms and deduce (
9.22
) from
[
63
, Corollary 2.13]
. Here we provide a straightforward proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{A}_{m-1}(u-1)\widetilde{A}_{m+1}(u)}{\widetilde{A}_{m}(u-1)\widetilde{A}_{m}(u)}\bigg\rvert_{\text{highest weight vector}}=\frac{P_{m}(u-1)}{P_{m}(u)}.

(9.24)

By Remark
, the highest weight vector is
V_{\Lambda^{0}}
where
\Lambda^{0}
is given by (
). According to Theorem
9.2
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\widetilde{A}_{m-1}(u-1)\widetilde{A}_{m+1}(u)}{\widetilde{A}_{m}(u-1)\widetilde{A}_{m}(u)}\bigg\rvert_{V_{\Lambda^{0}}}&=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{u-1-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m,i}+i}{u-1-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+1,i}+i}\times\frac{u-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+2,i}+i}{u-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+1,i}+i}\right)\\
\text{\small for $L\gg 0$}\quad&=\prod_{i=1}^{nL+r}\left(\frac{u-1-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m,i}+i}{u-1-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+1,i}+i}\times\frac{u-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+2,i}+i}{u-r-\Lambda^{0}_{m+1,i}+i}\right)\\
&=\frac{Q_{m}(u-1)_{L}}{Q_{m}(u)_{L}}\times\frac{Q_{m+1}(u+1)_{L}}{Q_{m+1}(u)_{L}},\end{split}

(9.25)

where

\displaystyle Q_{m}(u)_{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j)\in\mathrm{SYD}(\Lambda^{0}_{m}/\Lambda^{0}_{m+1})\\
i\leq nL+r\end{subarray}}(u-r+i-j).

(9.26)

Using the formula (
) for
\Lambda^{0}
, we observe that there is an equality between box configurations (see
[
63
, Lemma 2.6]
for the dual statement):

\displaystyle\mathrm{SYD}(\Lambda^{0}_{m}/\Lambda^{0}_{m+1})=\left\{(i,j)\>|\>(i,j)\text{ is a top box of a height $m$ column in }\mathrm{SYD}(\Lambda^{0}_{1}/\Lambda^{0}_{m+1})\right\}.

(9.27)

Whence it follows that:

\displaystyle Q_{m}(u)_{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(i,j)$ is a top box of}\\
\text{a height $m$ column}\\
\text{in }\mathrm{SYD}(\Lambda^{0}_{1}/\Lambda^{0}_{m+1})\\
\text{and $i\leq nL+r$}\end{subarray}}(u-r+i-j).

(9.28)

Using (
9.28
) and (
9.27
), one arrives at

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{m}(u)_{L}}{Q_{m+1}(u+1)_{L}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(i,j)$ is a top box of}\\
\text{a height $m$ column}\\
\text{in }\mathrm{SYD}(\Lambda_{1}/\Lambda_{n+1})\end{subarray}}(u-r+i-j).

(9.29)

Plug (
9.29
) into (
9.25
), and compare with (
9.24
), we conclude that
P_{m}(u)
is given by (
9.22
).
∎

Remark 9.4
.

According to Corollary
8.3
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{(r)}_{\infty}(n,k)
is isomorphic to
L_{k\varpi_{r}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)_{k})\otimes\mathrm{Fock}_{kr}(\widehat{\gl}(1)_{kn})
as a
\widehat{\gl}(n)_{k}
module. Theorem
9.3
describes the decomposition of
L_{k\varpi_{r}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)_{k})\otimes\mathrm{Fock}_{kr}(\widehat{\gl}(1)_{kn})
with respect to the Yangian action given by
\prescript{\infty}{}{\widetilde{T}}(u)
. When
r=0
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{(0)}_{\infty}(n,k)
is the vacuum module for
\widehat{\gl}(n)_{k}
, and in this case our result recovers Uglov’s result in
[
41
, Theorem 1.2]
.

9.3
Solving a higher-spin generalization of Calogero-Sutherland model

Recall that in Section
6.1
we defined a higher-spin analog of Calogero-Sutherland Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{CS}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Delta^{-1}(x_{i}\partial_{i})^{2}\Delta-2\sum_{i<j}^{N}\frac{x_{i}x_{j}(\Omega_{ij}+k)}{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}-(N-1)\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}\partial_{i}-\frac{N(N-1)(2N-1)}{6}.

(9.30)

H_{\mathrm{CS}}
is the Calogero representation of
\mathrm{Tr}((XY)^{2})
(Lemma
6.1
). Using the technique that we developed in previous sections, we can completely solve this Hamiltonian.

Theorem 9.4
.

The eigenspace decomposition of
\mathcal{H}_{N}(n,k)
with respect to the action of
H_{\mathrm{CS}}=\mathrm{Tr}((XY)^{2})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{N}(n,k)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda),

(9.31)

where the sum is taken for all
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
such that
\lambda
is admissible (Definition
8.6
), and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is defined in Definition
8.4
. The eigenvalue of
H_{\mathrm{CS}}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}-k)(\lambda_{i}-k+N+1-2i).

(9.32)

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)=\left(V^{N+n}_{\lambda}\otimes(V^{N}_{\lambda})^{*}\right)^{\GL_{N}^{\mathrm{diag}},-k}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is a subspace of the summand
M^{\lambda}_{\mu,\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}\otimes V^{n}_{\mu}\otimes V^{N}_{\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}\otimes(V^{N}_{\lambda})^{*}
in the decomposition

\displaystyle V^{N+n}_{\lambda}\otimes(V^{N}_{\lambda})^{*}=\bigoplus_{\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}M^{\lambda}_{\mu,\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}\otimes V^{n}_{\mu}\otimes V^{N}_{\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}\otimes(V^{N}_{\lambda})^{*}.

(9.33)

We note that
\gl_{N}
acts on
V^{N}_{\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}}
in the above decomposition via the generator
XY
. Therefore the quadratic Casimir of
\gl_{N}
, which is
\mathrm{Tr}((XY)^{2})
, acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
as the scalar
11
11

11

2\rho_{N}=
sum of positive roots of
\gl_{N}=(N-1,N-3,\cdots,3-N,1-N)
.

\displaystyle\langle\lambda^{\shortdownarrow},\lambda^{\shortdownarrow}+2\rho_{N}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}-k)(\lambda_{i}-k+N+1-2i).

(9.34)

∎

Remark 9.5
.

The same argument as the proof of Theorem
9.4
shows that the energy grading operator
\mathrm{Tr}(XY)
acts on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
as the scalar
d(\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lambda_{i}-k)
, therefore
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is homogeneous of degree
d(\lambda)
with respect to the original energy grading (
4.14
). In particular, the lowest energy subspace with respect to the Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{CS}}
is exactly the space of ground states
\mathcal{H}_{N}(n,k)_{0}
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{N}(n,k)_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda_{\min}),\quad\lambda_{\min,j}=k\lfloor\frac{N+n-j}{n}\rfloor.

(9.35)

Using the shifted energy grading (see Definition
7.1
),
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{N}(\lambda)
is homogeneous of degree

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{\min,j}\right)

(9.36)

Taking
N\rightarrow\infty
, the summand
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda)
in (
9.21
) is homogeneous of degree

\displaystyle{d}_{\infty}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\lambda_{j}+k\lfloor\frac{j-1-r}{n}\rfloor\right)

(9.37)

with respect to the shifted energy grading.

Appendix A
Hall-Littlewood Polynomial

In this appendix we review the basics of the Hall-Littlewood polynomial and its transformed version, following
[
66
, Section 3]
12
12

12

The variable
q
here is denoted by
t
there.
.

Definition A.1
.

For a Young diagram
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, we denote its associated partition by
(1^{\alpha_{1}},2^{\alpha_{2}},\cdots)
, then the Hall-Littlewood polynomial
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a};q)
in the variables
\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}
and
q
is defined by the formula

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a};q):=\frac{1}{\prod_{i\geq 0}[\alpha_{i}]_{q}!}\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\left(\mathbf{a}^{\lambda}\prod_{i<j}\frac{1-q\mathbf{a}_{j}/\mathbf{a}_{i}}{1-\mathbf{a}_{j}/\mathbf{a}_{i}}\right).

(A.1)

Here
\alpha_{0}=n-\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}
, and
\mathbf{a}^{\lambda}=\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots\mathbf{a}_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
, and we use the following
q
-number notation

\displaystyle[n]_{q}=\frac{1-q^{n}}{1-q},\;[n]_{q}!=[n]_{q}[n-1]_{q}\cdots[1]_{q},\;\left[\begin{matrix}n\\
k\end{matrix}\right]_{q}=\frac{[n]_{q}!}{[k]_{q}![n-k]_{q}!}.

The Hall-Littlewood polynomial
P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a};q)
is an interpolation between Schur symmetric functions
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a})
and monomial symmetric functions
m_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a})
, in fact we have

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a};0)=s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}),\;P_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a};1)=m_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}).

(A.2)

Definition A.2
.

For a pair of partitions
(\lambda,\mu)
, we define the Kostka-Foulkes functions
K_{\lambda\mu}(q)\in\mathbb{Q}(q)
to be coefficient of the expansion

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{\mu}K_{\lambda\mu}(q)P_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q).

(A.3)

It is true (but not obvious) that

\displaystyle K_{\lambda\mu}(q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[q],

i.e. Kostka-Foulkes functions are polynomials in
q
with non-negative integer coefficients, see
[
66
, Theorem 3.4.15]
.

In
[
23
]
, a transformed version of Hall-Littlewood polynomials is defined using the Jing operator, which we recall its definition here.

Definition A.3
.

For an integer
m
, the
m
-th Jing operator
S^{q}_{m}
is a linear map from
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n},q]
to itself, defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n},q]\ni f(\mathbf{a};q)\mapstochar\rightarrow(S^{q}_{m}f)(\mathbf{a};q):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,q\mathbf{a}_{i},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n};q)\frac{\mathbf{a}_{i}^{m}}{\prod_{j\neq i}(1-\mathbf{a}_{j}/\mathbf{a}_{i})}.

(A.4)

Remark A.1
.

The original definition of Jing operator in
[
23
]
(see
[
66
, Definition 3.4.5]
) is in terms of generating series
S^{q}(u)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{q}_{m}u^{m}
. In other words,
S^{q}(u)
is defined beforehand, and
S^{q}_{m}
in (
A.4
) is read out from the mode expansion of
S(u)
, see
[
53
, Lemma A.1]
. The operator
S(u)
is constructed such that it maps symmetric functions to symmetric functions, i.e.
S^{q}_{m}
maps
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}]^{S_{n}}[q]
to itself for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition A.4
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l}
be a Young diagram, define the transformed Hall-Littlewood polynomial
H_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q)\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}]^{S_{n}}[q]
by

\displaystyle H_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q)=S^{q}_{\mu_{1}}S^{q}_{\mu_{2}}\cdots S^{q}_{\mu_{l}}(1).

(A.5)

Theorem A.1

(see
[
66
, Corollary 3.4.12]
)
.

H_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q)
is related to the Schur functions by

\displaystyle H_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q)=\sum_{\lambda}K_{\lambda\mu}(q)s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{a}).

(A.6)

Appendix B
Affine Grassmannian

In this appendix we review the basics of affine Grassmannian and the Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian, following
[
50
]
. Then we give a geometric description of the Jing operator
S^{q}_{m}
in terms of convolution product of coherent sheaves on affine Grassmannian for
\GL_{n}
in Section
B.2
.

Let us denote
\mathscr{K}=\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!)
and
\mathscr{O}=\mathbb{C}[\![z]\!]
. For a reductive group
G
, we define the affine Grassmannian

\displaystyle\Gr_{G}:=G(\mathscr{K})/G(\mathscr{O}),

(B.1)

i.e. the coset of group of Laurent power series valued in
G
modulo subgroup of formal power series valued in
G
.
G(\mathscr{O})
acts from the left on
\Gr_{G}
, and
G(\mathscr{O})
-orbits are one-to-one correspond to dominant coweights of
G

[
50
, (2.1.2)]
:

\displaystyle\Gr_{G}=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{G}^{+}}\Gr^{\lambda}_{G},\quad\text{where }\;\Gr^{\lambda}_{G}:=G(\mathscr{O})z^{\lambda}G(\mathscr{O})/G(\mathscr{O}).

(B.2)

Here
\Lambda_{G}
is the coweight lattice of
G
and
\Lambda_{G}^{+}
is its dominant part. We note that
\mathbb{G}_{m}(\mathscr{K})
has a distinct point which corresponds to the completion morphism
\mathbb{C}[z^{\pm}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!)
, and
z^{\lambda}
is the image of the aforementioned distinct point in
G(\mathscr{K})
under the morphism
\lambda:\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow G
.

\Gr_{G}
is endowed with an ind-scheme structure, i.e.
\Gr_{G}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-points of a contravariant functor (still denote it by
\Gr_{G}
) from the category of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras to the category of sets, such that
\Gr_{G}
is isomorphic to the functor
\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}{\lim}X_{i}
for some index set
I
and schemes
X_{i}
where
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
is a closed embedding for every arrow
i\rightarrow j
in
I
. The functor
\Gr_{G}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\text{-Alg}\ni R\mapstochar\rightarrow\Gr_{G}(R)=\left\{(\mathcal{E},\phi)\>\bigg\rvert\>\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}\text{ is a principal $G$-bundle on }\mathbb{D}_{R}\\
\phi:\mathcal{E}|_{\mathbb{D}^{*}_{R}}\cong\mathcal{E}^{0}|_{\mathbb{D}^{*}_{R}}\text{ is a trivialization}\end{subarray}\right\}.

(B.3)

Here
\mathbb{D}_{R}:=\Spec R[\![z]\!]
and
\mathbb{D}^{*}_{R}:=\Spec R(\!(z)\!)
, and
\mathcal{E}^{0}
is the trivial principal
G
-bundle on
\mathbb{D}_{R}
.

Using the aforementioned ind-scheme structure, the
G(\mathscr{O})
-orbit
\Gr^{\lambda}_{G}
in
B.2
is a finite dimensional smooth subvariety and its Zariski closure
\bGr^{\lambda}_{G}
(endowed with reduced scheme structure) is a projective variety
[
50
, Proposition 2.1.5]
, which is called the (spherical) Schubert variety. Moreover, it is known that
[
50
, Proposition 2.1.4]
:

\displaystyle\bGr^{\lambda}_{G}=\bigcup_{\mu\leq\lambda}\Gr^{\mu}_{G}.

(B.4)

Here
\leq
is the Bruhat order, that is,
\mu\leq\lambda
if and only if
\lambda-\mu
is a non-negative integral linear combinations of simple coroots.

We note that
\mathbb{D}_{R}
can be regarded as a
\Spec R
-family of formal disks which is the germ of a smooth curve. Using a theorem of Beauville-Laszlo, the functor (
B.3
) can be extend to a smooth curve from germ of a point. Namely, we have functorial isomorphism
[
50
, Theorem 1.4.2]

\displaystyle\Gr_{G}(R)\cong\left\{(\mathcal{E},\phi)\>\bigg\rvert\>\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}\text{ is a principal $G$-bundle on }C_{R}\\
\phi:\mathcal{E}|_{C^{*}_{R}}\cong\mathcal{E}^{0}|_{C^{*}_{R}}\text{ is a trivialization}\end{subarray}\right\},

(B.5)

where
C
is a smooth algebraic curve with a distinct pint
x\in C
and
C^{*}:=C\setminus x
.

If we generalize (
B.5
) to allow multiple points and furthermore allow them to vary and collide, we arrive at the following.

Definition B.1
.

Let
C
be a smooth curve and
I
be a finite index set, then the Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian
\Gr_{G,C^{I}}
is defined to be the following contravariant functor

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\text{-Alg}\ni R\mapstochar\rightarrow\Gr_{G,C^{I}}(R)=\left\{(\mathcal{E},D,\phi)\>\bigg\rvert\>\begin{subarray}{c}D\in C^{I}(R),\;\mathcal{E}\text{ is a principal $G$-bundle on }C_{R}\\
\phi:\>\mathcal{E}|_{C_{R}\setminus D}\cong\mathcal{E}^{0}|_{C_{R}\setminus D}\text{ is a trivialization}\end{subarray}\right\}.

(B.6)

Here we identify
D\in C^{I}(R)
with an
I
-colored divisor on
C_{R}
. We define the symmetrized Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian
\Gr_{G,C^{(I)}}
to be the functor (
B.6
) with
D\in C^{I}(R)
replaced by
D\in\mathrm{Sym}^{|I|}C(R)
, that is, we identify
D
with an divisor of degree
|I|
on
C_{R}
.

We note that there are natural projections:

\displaystyle\Gr_{G,C^{I}}\rightarrow C^{I},\text{ and }\Gr_{G,C^{(I)}}\rightarrow C^{(I)}=\mathrm{Sym}^{|I|}C,

(B.7)

which map
(\mathcal{E},D,\phi)
to
D
. We call them the structure maps for (symmetrized) Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian.

It is known that
\Gr_{G,C^{I}}
and
\Gr_{G,C^{(I)}}
are ind-schemes
[
50
, Theorem 3.1.3]
.

\Gr_{G,C^{I}}
satisfies the following factorization property
[
50
, Theorem 3.2.1(iii)]
. Given
f:J\twoheadrightarrow I
, let
J=\coprod_{i\in I}J_{i}
denote the partition of
J
, and
C^{J/I}\subset C^{J}
denote the open subset of those
\{x_{j},j\in J\}
such that
x_{j}\cap x_{j^{\prime}}=\emptyset
if
f(j)\neq f(j^{\prime})
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\Gr_{G,C^{J}}\times_{C^{J}}C^{J/I}\cong\left(\prod_{i\in I}\Gr_{G,C^{J_{i}}}\right)\times_{C^{J}}C^{J/I}.

(B.8)

In particular, for the identity map
J\rightarrow J
we have
C^{J/J}=C^{J}\setminus\Delta
where
\Delta
is the divisor of those
\{x_{j},j\in J\}
such that
x_{j}=x_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j\neq j^{\prime}
, and (
B.8
) reads:

\displaystyle\Gr_{G,C^{J}}\times_{C^{J}}(C^{J}\setminus\Delta)\cong\left(\Gr_{G,C}\right)^{J}\times_{C^{J}}(C^{J}\setminus\Delta).

(B.9)

(
B.9
) also holds for symmtrized version:

\displaystyle\Gr_{G,C^{(J)}}\times_{C^{(J)}}(C^{(J)}\setminus\Delta)\cong\left(\Gr_{G,C}\right)^{(J)}\times_{C^{(J)}}(C^{(J)}\setminus\Delta),

(B.10)

where we still use
\Delta
to denote its image in the symmetrization
C^{(J)}
. In the case of
C=\mathbb{A}^{1}
, the coordinate
z
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
induces a coordinate for the formal disk
\mathbb{D}_{x}
for every
x\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
, so we have
\Gr_{G,\mathbb{A}^{1}}\cong\Gr_{G}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\Gr_{G,\mathbb{A}^{1}}\right)^{J}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{J}}(\mathbb{A}^{J}\setminus\Delta)&\cong\left(\Gr_{G}\right)^{J}\times(\mathbb{A}^{J}\setminus\Delta),\\
\left(\Gr_{G,\mathbb{A}^{1}}\right)^{(J)}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{(J)}}(\mathbb{A}^{(J)}\setminus\Delta)&\cong\left(\Gr_{G}\right)^{J}\times(\mathbb{A}^{(J)}\setminus\Delta).\end{split}

(B.11)

Definition B.2
.

For a
I
-tuple of dominant coweights
\{\lambda_{i},i\in I\}
We define the Beilinson-Drinfeld Schubert variety
\bGr^{\{\lambda_{i},i\in I\}}_{G,\mathbb{A}^{I}}
to be the Zariski closure of
\left(\prod_{i\in I}\bGr^{\lambda_{i}}_{G}\right)\times(\mathbb{A}^{I}\setminus\Delta)
in
\Gr_{G,\mathbb{A}^{I}}
, endowed with reduced scheme structure. The symmetrized version
\bGr^{\{\lambda_{i},i\in I\}}_{G,\mathbb{A}^{(I)}}
is defined to be the Zariski closure of
\left(\prod_{i\in I}\bGr^{\lambda_{i}}_{G}\right)\times(\mathbb{A}^{(I)}\setminus\Delta)
in
\Gr_{G,\mathbb{A}^{(I)}}
, endowed with reduced scheme structure.

Remark B.1
.

For a general smooth curve
C
, one can define the Beilinson-Drinfeld Schubert varieties
\bGr^{\{\lambda_{i},i\in I\}}_{G,\mathbb{A}^{I}}
and its symmetrized version
\bGr^{\{\lambda_{i},i\in I\}}_{G,\mathbb{A}^{(I)}}
using a twist of the direct product
\left(\prod_{i\in I}\bGr^{\lambda_{i}}_{G}\right)\times(\mathbb{A}^{I}\setminus\Delta)
by a principal
\Aut(\mathbb{D})^{I}
-bundle, see
[
50
, (3.1.10)]
.

B.1
The affine Grassmannian for
\GL_{n}

For
G=\GL_{n}
, a principal
\GL_{n}
-bundle is equivalent to a rank
n
vector bundle, so we can rewrite (
B.3
) and (
B.6
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Gr_{\GL_{n}}(R)=\left\{\mathcal{E}\>\bigg\rvert\>\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}\subset j_{*}\mathcal{O}^{\oplus n}_{\mathbb{D}^{*}_{R}}\text{ is a rank $n$ }\\
\text{locally free sub-sheaf}\end{subarray}\right\},\;j:\mathbb{D}^{*}_{R}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{R}\text{ is the embedding},

(B.12)

\displaystyle\Gr_{\GL_{n},C^{I}}(R)=\left\{(\mathcal{E},D)\>\bigg\rvert\>\begin{subarray}{c}D\in C^{I}(R),\;\mathcal{E}\subset j^{D}_{*}\mathcal{O}^{\oplus n}_{C_{R}\setminus D}\text{ is a }\\
\text{rank $n$ locally free sub-sheaf }\end{subarray}\right\},\;j^{D}:C_{R}\setminus D\hookrightarrow C_{R}\text{ is the embedding}.

(B.13)

In particular, a
\mathbb{C}
-point in
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
represents a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathscr{K}^{\oplus n}
, that is,
\Lambda
is required to be a rank
n
free module over
\mathscr{O}
.

Using Gauss elimination algorithm, one can show that there exists
g\in\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
such that
g\cdot\Lambda=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}z^{\lambda_{i}}\mathscr{O}
where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is a dominant coweight of
\GL_{n}
.
\lambda
is uniquely determined by
\Lambda
, and this is exactly the decomposition (
B.2
) in the case when
G=\GL_{n}
.

Example B.1
.

\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
is defined to be the
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
-orbit through
z^{\omega_{1}}
which represents the lattice
z\mathscr{O}\oplus\mathscr{O}^{\oplus(n-1)}
. Note that
(z\mathscr{O})^{\oplus n}\subset z\mathscr{O}\oplus\mathscr{O}^{\oplus(n-1)}\subset\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}
, and the
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
-action preserves these inclusions, that is,
(z\mathscr{O})^{\oplus n}\subset\Lambda\subset\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}
for every
\Lambda\in\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
. Therefore
\Lambda
is uniquely determined by
n-1
dimensional linear subspace of
(\mathscr{O}/z\mathscr{O})^{\oplus n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
, whence
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. In particular, we see that
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
is proper, thus
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}=\bGr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
.

Example B.2
.

The previous example shows that
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
is the moduli space of
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}
such that
\dim\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}/\mathcal{E}=1
. This can be generalized to
\bGr^{N\omega_{1}}_{\GL_{n}}
: it is the moduli space of
\mathcal{E}\subset\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}
such that
\dim\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}/\mathcal{E}=N

[
50
, Example 2.1.8]
.

A connected component of
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
is indexed by an integer
N\in\mathbb{Z}
, and is denoted by
\Gr^{(N)}_{\GL_{n}}
. It is known that a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathscr{K}^{\oplus n}
is in
\Gr^{(N)}_{\GL_{n}}
if and only if
[
55
, 4.1]

\displaystyle\dim(\mathscr{O}^{\oplus n}/z^{M}\Lambda)=N+Mn,\quad\forall M\gg 0.

In terms of
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
-orbit, we have

\displaystyle\Gr^{(N)}_{\GL_{n}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}=N\end{subarray}}\Gr^{\lambda}_{\GL_{n}}.

(B.14)

B.2
Geometric description of Jing operator

In this section we give a geometric description of Jing operators
S^{q}_{m}
, and outine a proof of
[
53
, Corollary B.3]
.

Consider the affine Grassmannian
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}=\GL_{n}(\mathscr{K})/\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
, and let
\omega_{1}=(1,0,\cdots,0)
be the first fundamental coweight of
\GL_{n}
, then the
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
-orbit
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
and it is fixed by the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-rotation.

There is a convolution product on affine Grassmannian:

\displaystyle m:\Gr_{\GL_{n}}\widetilde{\times}\Gr_{\GL_{n}}=\GL_{n}(\mathscr{K})\overset{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})}{\times}\GL_{n}(\mathscr{K})/\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rightarrow\GL_{n}(\mathscr{K})/\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O}).

(B.15)

Here the map sends
(g_{1},g_{2})
to
g_{1}g_{2}
.

Consider
D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})
, the
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-equivariant bounded derive category of coherent sheaves on
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
. Here coherent sheaves on the ind-scheme
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
are defined to have finite type support. The convolution map of
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
induces a functor

\displaystyle\star:D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})\times D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})\rightarrow D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\star\mathcal{G}=Rm_{*}(\mathcal{F}\widetilde{\boxtimes}\mathcal{G}).

Passing to the
K
-theory, we obtain an map

\displaystyle\star:K_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})\otimes K_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow K_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}}).

(B.16)

It is known that the
\star
-product on
K_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})
is associative, and the pushforward of the structure sheaf of origin
1\in\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
is an identity element for
\star
.

The determinant line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\Gr_{\GL_{n}}

[
50
, 1.5]
is
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-equivariant. Let us use
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(m)
to denote
i_{*}i^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)^{\otimes m}
where
i:\Gr^{\omega_{1}}\hookrightarrow\Gr_{\GL_{n}}
is the natural embedding. Since
i
is
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-equivariant,
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(m)
is also
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-equivariant.

For an object
\mathcal{F}\in D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})
, we denote its
\GL_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
-equivariant Euler characteristic by
\chi_{q,\mathbf{a}}(\mathcal{F})\in K_{\GL_{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\mathrm{pt})=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{a}_{1}^{\pm},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}^{\pm}]^{S_{n}}[q^{\pm}]
. The following result states that the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(m)\star(-)
is a geometrization of the Jing operator
S^{q}_{m}
.

Proposition B.1

(see
[
53
, Corollary B.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in D^{b}_{\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}(\Gr_{\GL_{n}})
, then

\displaystyle\chi_{q,\mathbf{a}}(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(m)\star\mathcal{F})=S^{q}_{m}\left(\chi_{q,\mathbf{a}}(\mathcal{F})\right).

(B.17)

The idea of proof of Proposition
B.1
is to identify the right-hand-side of (
A.4
) as the Atiyah-Bott localization formula for
\Gr^{\omega_{1}}
.

Combine (
B.17
) with the definition of
H_{\mu}
in terms of iterative action of
S_{\mu_{i}}
(
A.5
), and we obtain the following.

Corollary B.2

(see
[
53
, Corollary B.2]
)
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{l}
be a Young diagram, then

\displaystyle H_{\mu}(\mathbf{a};q)=\chi_{q,\mathbf{a}}(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(\mu_{1})\star\cdots\star\mathcal{O}_{\Gr^{\omega_{1}}}(\mu_{l})).

(B.18)

Finally, the following result is deduced from Corollary
B.2
by taking the spacial case
\mu_{1}=\cdots=\mu_{N}=k
.

Corollary B.3

(see
[
53
, Corollary B.3]
)
.

Let
\overline{\Gr}^{N\omega_{1}}
be the closure of the
\GL_{n}(\mathscr{O})
-orbit through
z^{N\omega_{1}}
, then

\displaystyle\chi_{q,\mathbf{a}}(\overline{\Gr}^{N\omega_{1}},\mathcal{O}(k))=H_{(k^{N})}(\mathbf{a};q).

(B.19)

Here
(k^{N})
is the partition consisting of
N
copies of
k
.

The key idea of the proof of Corollary
B.3
is to use the isomorphism
m^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)\cong\mathcal{O}(1)\widetilde{\boxtimes}\cdots\widetilde{\boxtimes}\mathcal{O}(1)
and rationality of the singularities of
\overline{\Gr}^{N\omega_{1}}
, i.e.
Rm_{*}\mathcal{O}\cong\mathcal{O}
. See
[
53
, Corollary B.3]
for the details.

Appendix C
Identities

Let
f(x,y),g(x,y)
be two polynomials of non-commutative variables
(x,y)
, then we have

A^{a}f(X,Y)[X,Y]g(X,Y)B_{b}\\
=A^{a}f(X,Y)B_{c}A^{c}g(X,Y)B_{b}-(k+n)A^{a}f(X,Y)g(X,Y)B_{b},

(C.3)

\mathrm{Tr}(f(X,Y)[X,Y]g(X,Y))\\
=\mathrm{Tr}(f(X,Y)B_{c}A^{c}g(X,Y))-(k+n)\mathrm{Tr}(f(X,Y)g(X,Y))-\mathrm{Tr}(f(X,Y))\mathrm{Tr}(g(X,Y)).

(C.6)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(X^{r}[X,Y]X^{s})=-\mathrm{Tr}(X^{r})\mathrm{Tr}(X^{s}).

(C.7)

Proof.

For a family of operator
\{\mathcal{O}^{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
transforming in the
\gl_{N}
vector representation, we have (
8.14
):

\displaystyle[X,Y]^{i}_{j}\mathcal{O}^{j}=(BA)^{i}_{j}\mathcal{O}^{j}-(n+k)\mathcal{O}^{i}.

(
C.3
) is obtained by applying the above equation to
\mathcal{O}=g(X,Y)B_{b}
.

For a family of operator
\{\mathcal{A}^{i}_{j}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
transforming in the
\gl_{N}
adjoint representation, we have:

\displaystyle[\mu^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{i}_{j}),\mathcal{A}^{j}_{l}]=N\mathcal{A}^{i}_{l}-\delta^{i}_{l}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{j}.

Using the identity
\mu^{*}_{\mathbb{C}}(E^{i}_{j})=(XY)^{i}_{j}-(YX)^{i}_{j}-(BA)^{i}_{j}+(N+n)\delta^{i}_{j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle[X,Y]^{i}_{j}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{l}=(BA)^{i}_{j}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{l}-(n+k)\mathcal{A}^{i}_{l}-\delta^{i}_{l}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{j}.

(
C.6
) is obtained by applying the above equation to
\mathcal{A}=g(X,Y)
.
∎
